\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}

Finite-state symbolic models impose rigid constraints on counting
functions. In a deterministic automaton, the number of accepted words
of length \(m\) is governed by powers of a finite matrix. In a sofic
shift, the number of admissible words of a given length is likewise
controlled by a finite-state presentation. These mechanisms force
periodic density laws or exponential-polynomial growth, and that is
often enough to rule out arithmetic languages whose size is governed by
the prime number theorem.

This paper isolates a quantitative finite-budget obstruction. The first
part makes the fixed-base obstruction effective: from the DFA transition
matrix and explicit prime-counting bounds one computes the residue
alternatives, thresholds, and constants in the base-\(b\) prime-slice
comparison. The second part removes the strongly connected restriction
from the relative-density comparison. For an arbitrary trim
deterministic presentation with Perron root \(\lambda>1\), possibly
with several maximal strongly connected components and transient paths
between them, the Perron-scale residue classes have residue-wise
expansions with polynomial factors, while the remaining classes are
sub-Perron. Comparing the active polynomial exponents gives a
finite-budget obstruction for target families of size
\(\asymp\lambda^m/m\) on those active classes. The Zeckendorf
prime-slice results are then the strongly connected specialization of
this mechanism.

The language-theoretic part is independent of the prime number theorem.
After the Zeckendorf context-free argument, it combines the weak pumping
lemma for multiple context-free languages with the eventual affine
recurrence action. Every infinite MCFL then contains one increasing
orbit that returns simultaneously modulo every integer coprime to the
trailing recurrence coefficient. This yields a copy of \(\mathbb Q\) in
the associated deleted-congruence topology, a Cantor closure in the
completion, and an exact Cantor--Bendixson obstruction. Prime and
bounded-\(\Omega\) representation languages are MCF-immune even in the
nonunit case. In a unit recurrence witness, arbitrary-depth quotient
congruences produce an induced countably branching divisibility tree.
The trailing coefficient of a nonminimal witness is not a
numeration-system invariant: \Cref{prop:recurrence-witness-separation} exhibits
ordinary base \(p\) and an inflated Zeckendorf witness with the same
selected bad-prime set but opposite escape behavior.
Passing instead to the intrinsic minimal tail recurrence yields an exact
inverse statement: bounded prime support away from its constant term
occurs on an infinite MCFL precisely when the canonical language
contains a synchronized ray whose values are an integer geometric
progression. An explicit linear MCFG generates every such ray. When the
canonical syntax is supplied by a DFA, positive instances are
semidecidable; no general decision procedure is asserted.

For explicit Zeckendorf constants we use the canonical trim strongly
connected presentation, but the abstract finite-budget comparison in
\Cref{thm:multicomponent-finite-budget} applies to arbitrary trim
deterministic presentations. Numeration automata with multiple strongly
connected components do occur
\cite{CharlierRampersadRigoWaxweiler2010}; their polynomial factors are
accounted for in that theorem rather than excluded from the framework.

Throughout, \(f(m)\asymp g(m)\) means that \(c_1g(m)\le f(m)\le c_2g(m)\)
for some positive constants \(c_1,c_2\) and all sufficiently large
\(m\). We use the standard prime-counting convention
\(\pi(x)=\#\{p\le x:p\text{ prime}\}\). Thus an integer half-open
interval \([X,Y)\) contains exactly \(\pi(Y-1)-\pi(X-1)\) primes; in
asymptotic estimates below this may equivalently be written
\(\pi(Y)-\pi(X)+O(1)\). We write \(F_0=0\), \(F_1=1\) for the Fibonacci numbers,
\(\varphi=(1+\sqrt5)/2\), and for a Zeckendorf word
\(w=x_1\cdots x_m\) we set
\[
  \val(w):=\sum_{k=1}^m x_kF_{k+1}.
\]
For \(n\ge2\), \(\omega(n)\) denotes the number of distinct rational
prime divisors of \(n\), while \(\Omega(n)\) denotes the total number
of prime factors of \(n\), counted with multiplicity. We put
\(\omega(1)=\Omega(1)=0\), so \(\omega(n)\le\Omega(n)\) for all
\(n\ge1\).

\subsection{Main results}

We separate the classical background inputs from the quantitative
corollaries proved later; the precise residue-class refinements and
proofs appear in Sections~\ref{sec:automata}--\ref{sec:zeckendorf}.

The logical chain is the following. First,
\Cref{thm:dfa-density-dichotomy} supplies the residue constants \(c_r\)
for a fixed complete DFA, and
\Cref{thm:multicomponent-finite-budget} supplies the multicomponent
finite-budget comparison for general trim deterministic presentations.
Second,
\Cref{lem:explicit-pnt-input,lem:base-prime-count} turn a published
explicit prime-counting estimate into concrete base-\(b\) slice
constants \(A_b,B_b,M_b\). Third,
\Cref{thm:effective-fixed-dfa-prime-obstruction} combines these two
finite inputs and outputs the certified residue alternatives,
thresholds, and symmetric-difference and precision bounds. The later
base-\(b\) and Zeckendorf corollaries are consequences of this
finite-budget conversion.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{p{0.18\linewidth}p{0.72\linewidth}}
\textbf{Symbol} & \textbf{Meaning}\\
\(D_A,c_r\) & Period and residue density constants for a complete DFA
\(\mathcal A\) over \(\Sigma_b\).\\
\(\mathcal P_m^{(b)}\) & The base-\(b\) prime slice; we reserve
\(p_m^{(b)}=\abs{\mathcal P_m^{(b)}}\) for its size.\\
\(D_N,d_r,e_r\) & Period and numerator/denominator residue constants
for a trim strongly connected presentation \(\mathcal N\).\\
\(s_r,t_r,S,\lambda\) & Polynomial exponents, maximum exponent, and
Perron root in the multicomponent presentation theorem.\\
\(D_{\mathrm Z},c_r^{(\mathrm Z)}\) & Period and relative-density
constants for the Zeckendorf product presentation.\\
\(\mathcal P_m^{(\mathrm Z)}\), \(\mathcal P_{\mathcal Z}\) &
The length-\(m\) Zeckendorf prime slice and the full prime Zeckendorf
language.\\
\(A_b,B_b,M_b\), \(A_{\mathrm Z},B_{\mathrm Z},M_{\mathrm Z}\) &
Explicit prime-slice lower/upper constants and thresholds in the
base-\(b\) and Zeckendorf settings.\\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

We begin with the classical density dichotomy for deterministic
automata, recorded in \Cref{thm:dfa-density-dichotomy} because its
constants feed directly into the prime approximation bounds. In the
binary case, if
\[
  a_m(\mathcal A)
  :=\#\{w\in\{0,1\}^m:\mathcal A\text{ accepts }w\},
\]
then
\[
  \frac{a_m(\mathcal A)}{2^m}=c_{m\bmod D_A}+O(\theta^m)
\]
for some automaton period \(D_A\), constants \(c_r\ge0\) indexed by
\(r\bmod D_A\), and \(\theta\in(0,1)\). Thus, on each residue
class, the normalized count either decays exponentially or is eventually
bounded below by a positive constant. In particular, the intermediate scale
\(a_m(\mathcal A)/2^m\asymp m^{-\alpha}\) is excluded.

When this is applied to the binary prime language
\(\mathcal P_m^{(2)}\), whose size is \(\asymp 2^m/m\) by the prime
number theorem \cite{HardyWright2008}, the full base-\(b\) results
\Cref{cor:dfa-prime-symmetric-diff,cor:dfa-prime-recall-precision}
give the following binary consequences. For every fixed deterministic
finite automaton there is \(c>0\) such that
\[
  \abs{(L(\mathcal A)\cap\{0,1\}^m)\triangle\mathcal P_m^{(2)}}
  \ge c\,\frac{2^m}{m}
\]
for all sufficiently large \(m\), and positive-density languages have
\(\gg 2^m\) symmetric difference along an arithmetic progression. For
the corresponding recall and precision ratios, every fixed DFA has
either exponentially decaying recall or \(O(1/m)\) precision along an
infinite arithmetic progression. These introductory statements are
summaries of the later corollaries, not separate theorem content.

\begin{example}[MSD-first base-\(5\) convention]\label{ex:msd-base}
  With digits read from the most significant position first, the word
  \(w=2031\in\{0,1,2,3,4\}^4\) encodes the integer
  \(2\cdot 5^3+0\cdot 5^2+3\cdot 5+1=266\). The leading digit must be
  non-zero when counting prime slices of fixed length: although the
  word \(0031\) has the same numerical value, it is excluded from
  \(\mathcal P^{(5)}_4\) because it lies outside
  \(\{1,\dots,4\}\Sigma_5^{3}\). This is the convention used throughout
  \Cref{sec:automata}.
\end{example}

For the Zeckendorf setting we use the background growth law proved in
the specialized form needed in
\Cref{prop:zeckendorf-regular-powerlaw}. It applies to regular
sublanguages of
\[
  \mathcal Z
  :=\{x_1\cdots x_m\in\{0,1\}^\ast:
      x_ix_{i+1}\neq 11,\ x_m=1\}.
\]
For such an \(L\), after deleting states that are not reachable from
the initial state or cannot reach an accepting state, let \(\rho\) be
the maximal spectral radius of a strongly connected component in a DFA
recognizing \(L\), and define
\[
  N_L(T):=\#\{w\in L:\val(w)\le T\},
\]
where \(\val(w)\) is the Zeckendorf value of \(w\). The growth law says
\[
  N_L(T)=T^{\alpha+o(1)},
  \qquad
  \alpha=
  \begin{cases}
    0, & \rho\le 1,\\[0.3em]
    \dfrac{\log \rho}{\log \varphi}, & \rho>1.
  \end{cases}
  \qquad (T\to\infty).
\]
\Cref{sec:zeckendorf} combines this background statement with
prime-counting estimates to deduce the quantitative Zeckendorf
slice-obstruction corollaries. Its language-theoretic conclusions are
stronger and do not use the prime number theorem. The regular theorem
\Cref{thm:coprime-quotient-zeckendorf-pumping} and its context-free
extension
\Cref{thm:context-free-coprime-quotient-zeckendorf-pumping} prove, by
block-action pumping modulo \(N_i^2\), that every sufficiently long
word in the respective language class starts an infinite chain
\[
  N_{i+1}=N_iQ_i,\qquad Q_i\equiv1\pmod {N_i}.
\]
Thus each pumping step may be chosen with quotient coprime to the
previous value, and \(\omega(\val(w))\) is unbounded on every infinite
context-free \(L\subseteq\mathcal Z\). The general recurrent-system
results
\Cref{thm:mcfl-synchronized-congruence-orbit,thm:mcfl-local-adic-fingerprint,thm:nonunit-mcfl-escape-dichotomy,thm:bounded-outside-support-geometric-ray}
upgrade prime immunity to MCF-immunity and isolate the exact nonunit
escape alternative and its intrinsic geometric-ray inverse. The
positive semidecidability consequence is
\Cref{cor:bounded-outside-support-semidecidable}. In unit systems,
\Cref{thm:unit-mcfl-deep-congruence-chain,thm:unit-mcfl-divisibility-tree}
give arbitrary congruence depth and the induced divisibility tree.
The specialization to standard linear Pisot \(U\)-systems is
\Cref{cor:linear-pisot-prime-cf-immunity}. In the endpoint case \(\rho\le1\), the
displayed formula is used only in the form \(N_L(T)=T^{o(1)}\).

\begin{example}[LSD-first Zeckendorf convention]\label{ex:lsd-zeck}
  Consider the integer \(23=F_8+F_3\). Its Zeckendorf expansion uses
  digits \(x_k\in\{0,1\}\) with \(x_k\) multiplying \(F_{k+1}\) and the
  least significant digit listed first. The admissible word
  \(w=0100001\) has length \(7\), satisfies the no-\(11\) rule, and
  evaluates to
  \(x_1F_2+x_2F_3+\cdots+x_7F_8=0+1\cdot 2+\cdots+1\cdot 21=23\).
  The terminal \(1\) is the most significant digit, consistent with the
  trim deterministic presentation used in \Cref{sec:zeckendorf}.
\end{example}

The final remarks are only contextual. They recall the classical

 succeeded in 354ms:
  \[
    N_{i+1}=N_iQ_i,\qquad Q_i>1,\qquad
    Q_i\equiv1\pmod {M_iN_i^{r_i}}.
  \]
  If \(M_i\) is divisible by every prime at most \(B_i\), then every
  prime divisor of \(Q_i\) exceeds \(B_i\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Take the fixed orbit \(N(t)\) from
  \Cref{thm:mcfl-synchronized-congruence-orbit}, discarding finitely many
  initial parameters so that \(N(t)\ge2\). Having selected
  \(N_i=N(t_i)\), apply the synchronized congruence with
  \[
    q_i=M_iN_i^{r_i+1}.
  \]
  Set \(t_{i+1}=t_i+H(q_i)\). Strict increase and the congruence give
  \[
    N_{i+1}=N_i+c_iM_iN_i^{r_i+1}
           =N_i(1+c_iM_iN_i^{r_i})
  \]
  for an integer \(c_i\ge1\). This proves the first assertion. Under
  the last hypothesis, \(Q_i\equiv1\pmod p\) for every prime
  \(p\le B_i\), so none of those primes divides \(Q_i\).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Induced full divisibility tree]
  \label{thm:unit-mcfl-divisibility-tree}
  Under the hypotheses of
  \Cref{thm:unit-mcfl-deep-congruence-chain}, there is an injection
  \[
    \Phi:\NN^{<\omega}\longrightarrow L
  \]
  such that
  \[
    \val_U(\Phi(\sigma))\mid\val_U(\Phi(\tau))
      \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \sigma\preceq\tau,
  \]
  where \(\preceq\) denotes the prefix order. The quotients attached to
  distinct edges can be chosen pairwise coprime. Moreover, given an
  arbitrary prescribed threshold for each edge, every prime divisor of
  its quotient can be required to exceed that threshold.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Use the fixed strictly increasing orbit from
  \Cref{thm:mcfl-synchronized-congruence-orbit}. Enumerate the edges of
  \(\NN^{<\omega}\) so that each parent occurs before its children. Map
  the root to any orbit word of value \(R_0\ge2\). Suppose finitely many
  edges have been assigned, and consider a new edge from a parent of
  value \(P\). Let \(R\) be divisible by \(R_0\), by every previously
  assigned edge quotient, and by every prime not exceeding the prescribed
  threshold for the new edge. Apply the synchronized orbit congruence
  modulo \(P^2R\) at the parameter of the parent. A sufficiently large
  positive multiple of the return time gives an unused orbit word of
  value \(P'>P\) with
  \[
    P'\equiv P\pmod {P^2R}.
  \]
  Hence \(P'=PQ\), where \(Q>1\) and \(Q\equiv1\pmod {PR}\). In
  particular, \(Q\) is coprime to the root and to every earlier edge
  quotient, and no prime up to the prescribed threshold divides \(Q\).

  Recursion defines \(\Phi\) on every node. The value at a node is the
  root value times the product of the edge quotients along its path.
  Since the root and all distinct edge quotients are pairwise coprime,
  a node value divides another exactly when its path-edge set is an
  initial segment of the other path. This is precisely the prefix
  relation and also proves injectivity.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Prime immunity for recurrent numeration systems]
  \label{thm:recurrent-numeration-prime-cf-immunity}
  Let \((U,A,\mathcal R_U)\) satisfy \textup{(U1)--(U4)}, and put
  \[
    \mathcal P_U
      :=\{w\in\mathcal R_U:\val_U(w)\text{ is prime}\}.
  \]
  Then \(\mathcal P_U\) is MCF-immune, and therefore CF-immune,
  REG-immune, and not recognizable by a finite automaton over \(A\).

  If, in addition, \(\abs{a_0}=1\), then every infinite multiple
  context-free
  \(L\subseteq\mathcal R_U\) has unbounded
  \(\omega(\val_U(w))\). More precisely, \(L\) contains a
  coprime-quotient chain \(N_{i+1}=N_iQ_i\) with
  \(Q_i>1\) and \(Q_i\equiv1\pmod {N_i}\). Thus the bounded-\(\omega\),
  bounded-\(\Omega\), and finite-prime-support languages of a unit
  system are MCF-immune whenever they are infinite. Without the unit
  hypothesis, every bounded-\(\Omega\) language is still MCF-immune.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  The MCF-immunity assertions follow from
  \Cref{thm:nonunit-mcfl-escape-dichotomy}, including its
  bounded-\(\Omega\) conclusion. In a unit system \(S\) is empty, so
  that theorem gives unbounded \(\omega\). Taking
  \(M_i=r_i=1\) in
  \Cref{thm:unit-mcfl-deep-congruence-chain} gives the stated chain.
  Regular languages are context-free and context-free languages are
  MCFLs. Finally, \textup{(U2)} and Euclid's theorem make
  \(\mathcal P_U\) infinite.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Linear Pisot \(U\)-systems]
  \label{cor:linear-pisot-prime-cf-immunity}
  Let \(U=(U_n)_{n\ge0}\) be a linear Pisot \(U\)-system in the
  following precise sense: \(U_0=1\), the \(U_n\) are strictly increasing
  integers with bounded quotients, \(\mathcal R_U\) is the LSD-first
  reversal of the usual greedy representation language, and \(U\)
  eventually satisfies an integral recurrence whose characteristic
  polynomial is the minimal polynomial of a Pisot number. Then its prime
  representation language is MCF-immune, hence CF-immune, REG-immune and
  nonrecognizable. The same statements hold in the usual MSD-first
  convention.

  If the dominant Pisot number is a unit and the chosen recurrence has
  trailing coefficient \(a_0=\pm1\), then the full coprime-quotient and
  bounded-prime-support MCF-immunity conclusions of
  \Cref{thm:recurrent-numeration-prime-cf-immunity} hold.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
  Greedy expansion gives a unique representation of every positive
  integer, and its length-\(m\) words represent values between successive
  place-value thresholds; thus \textup{(U1)--(U3)} hold. The defining
  Pisot recurrence gives \textup{(U4)}. Apply
  \Cref{thm:recurrent-numeration-prime-cf-immunity}. Frougny's finite
  normalization theorem supplies a finite transducer for normalization
  in this Pisot setting, and the associated canonical syntax and
  Bertrand/substitution descriptions are standard
  \cite{Frougny1992,BruyereHansel1997,Fabre1995,FrougnySolomyak1992}.
  Those finite-state results identify this corollary with the usual
  recognizability convention; the immunity proof itself uses only
  \textup{(U1)--(U4)}. Finally, multiple context-free, context-free, and
  regular languages are closed under reversal, so changing between the LSD-first proof
  convention and the usual MSD-first convention preserves every
  conclusion.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Unit and nonunit Pisot systems]
  \label{ex:pisot-unit-nonunit}
  The Fibonacci recurrence has characteristic polynomial
  \(X^2-X-1\), the Pell recurrence has \(X^2-2X-1\), and the
  Tribonacci recurrence has \(X^3-X^2-X-1\). Their trailing recurrence
  coefficient is \(a_0=1\), so the iterated conclusion applies. In
  ordinary base \(b\), \(U_n=b^n\) and \(a_0=b\); prime MCF-immunity
  follows, recovering the classical conclusion, but the unit iteration
  is not being asserted. A genuinely nonintegral, nonunit Pisot example
  is the dominant root \(1+\sqrt3\) of \(X^2-2X-2\): the recurrence
  \(U_{n+2}=2U_{n+1}+2U_n\) has \(a_0=2\), and the action is singular
  modulo \(2\).
\end{example}

\begin{remark}[Exact open interfaces]
  \label{rem:pisot-open-interfaces}
  The terminology for Pisot numeration systems is not uniform.
  \Cref{cor:linear-pisot-prime-cf-immunity} covers the standard
  positional linear \(U\)-systems just specified, including integer
  bases, Zeckendorf/Fibonacci, Pell, and higher-order Pisot recurrences.
  It does not assert the theorem for arbitrary real-base
  \(\beta\)-shift representations, non-positional abstract numeration
  systems, redundant noninjective digit systems, or systems lacking an
  eventual integral recurrence and strict length ordering. Extending
  the argument to any of those classes requires a stationary finite
  block action that is invertible modulo represented primes.

  For a fixed nonunit recurrence witness, the ambient action can be
  singular modulo primes dividing \(a_0\), so the unrestricted unit-system
  divisibility tree need not follow. The witness-relative surviving
  statement is \Cref{thm:nonunit-mcfl-escape-dichotomy}: every infinite
  MCFL must acquire unbounded prime support outside that finite set or
  unbounded valuation at one of its primes. The invariant restricted
  action and the failure of arbitrary recurrence witnesses to classify
  escape behavior are distinguished in
  \Cref{prop:recurrence-witness-separation}.
  The ordinary-base example preceding
  \Cref{thm:unit-mcfl-deep-congruence-chain} shows that the valuation
  alternative cannot be removed.
\end{remark}

---
name: brainstorming
description: "You MUST use this before any creative work - creating features, building components, adding functionality, or modifying behavior. Explores user intent, requirements and design before implementation."
---

# Brainstorming Ideas Into Designs

Help turn ideas into fully formed designs and specs through natural collaborative dialogue.

Start by understanding the current project context, then ask questions one at a time to refine the idea. Once you understand what you're building, present the design and get user approval.

<HARD-GATE>
Do NOT invoke any implementation skill, write any code, scaffold any project, or take any implementation action until you have presented a design and the user has approved it. This applies to EVERY project regardless of perceived simplicity.
</HARD-GATE>

## Anti-Pattern: "This Is Too Simple To Need A Design"

Every project goes through this process. A todo list, a single-function utility, a config change — all of them. "Simple" projects are where unexamined assumptions cause the most wasted work. The design can be short (a few sentences for truly simple projects), but you MUST present it and get approval.

## Checklist

You MUST create a task for each of these items and complete them in order:

1. **Explore project context** — check files, docs, recent commits
2. **Offer visual companion** (if topic will involve visual questions) — this is its own message, not combined with a clarifying question. See the Visual Companion section below.
3. **Ask clarifying questions** — one at a time, understand purpose/constraints/success criteria
4. **Propose 2-3 approaches** — with trade-offs and your recommendation
5. **Present design** — in sections scaled to their complexity, get user approval after each section
6. **Write design doc** — save to `docs/superpowers/specs/YYYY-MM-DD-<topic>-design.md` and commit
7. **Spec self-review** — quick inline check for placeholders, contradictions, ambiguity, scope (see below)
8. **User reviews written spec** — ask user to review the spec file before proceeding
9. **Transition to implementation** — invoke writing-plans skill to create implementation plan

## Process Flow

```dot
digraph brainstorming {
    "Explore project context" [shape=box];
    "Visual questions ahead?" [shape=diamond];
    "Offer Visual Companion\n(own message, no other content)" [shape=box];
    "Ask clarifying questions" [shape=box];
    "Propose 2-3 approaches" [shape=box];
    "Present design sections" [shape=box];
    "User approves design?" [shape=diamond];
    "Write design doc" [shape=box];
    "Spec self-review\n(fix inline)" [shape=box];
    "User reviews spec?" [shape=diamond];
    "Invoke writing-plans skill" [shape=doublecircle];

    "Explore project context" -> "Visual questions ahead?";
    "Visual questions ahead?" -> "Offer Visual Companion\n(own message, no other content)" [label="yes"];
    "Visual questions ahead?" -> "Ask clarifying questions" [label="no"];
    "Offer Visual Companion\n(own message, no other content)" -> "Ask clarifying questions";
    "Ask clarifying questions" -> "Propose 2-3 approaches";
    "Propose 2-3 approaches" -> "Present design sections";
    "Present design sections" -> "User approves design?";
    "User approves design?" -> "Present design sections" [label="no, revise"];
    "User approves design?" -> "Write design doc" [label="yes"];
    "Write design doc" -> "Spec self-review\n(fix inline)";
    "Spec self-review\n(fix inline)" -> "User reviews spec?";
    "User reviews spec?" -> "Write design doc" [label="changes requested"];
    "User reviews spec?" -> "Invoke writing-plans skill" [label="approved"];
}
```

**The terminal state is invoking writing-plans.** Do NOT invoke frontend-design, mcp-builder, or any other implementation skill. The ONLY skill you invoke after brainstorming is writing-plans.

## The Process

**Understanding the idea:**

- Check out the current project state first (files, docs, recent commits)
- Before asking detailed questions, assess scope: if the request describes multiple independent subsystems (e.g., "build a platform with chat, file storage, billing, and analytics"), flag this immediately. Don't spend questions refining details of a project that needs to be decomposed first.
- If the project is too large for a single spec, help the user decompose into sub-projects: what are the independent pieces, how do they relate, what order should they be built? Then brainstorm the first sub-project through the normal design flow. Each sub-project gets its own spec → plan → implementation cycle.
- For appropriately-scoped projects, ask questions one at a time to refine the idea
- Prefer multiple choice questions when possible, but open-ended is fine too
- Only one question per message - if a topic needs more exploration, break it into multiple questions
- Focus on understanding: purpose, constraints, success criteria

**Exploring approaches:**

- Propose 2-3 different approaches with trade-offs
- Present options conversationally with your recommendation and reasoning
- Lead with your recommended option and explain why

**Presenting the design:**

- Once you believe you understand what you're building, present the design
- Scale each section to its complexity: a few sentences if straightforward, up to 200-300 words if nuanced
- Ask after each section whether it looks right so far
- Cover: architecture, components, data flow, error handling, testing
- Be ready to go back and clarify if something doesn't make sense

**Design for isolation and clarity:**

- Break the system into smaller units that each have one clear purpose, communicate through well-defined interfaces, and can be understood and tested independently
- For each unit, you should be able to answer: what does it do, how do you use it, and what does it depend on?
- Can someone understand what a unit does without reading its internals? Can you change the internals without breaking consumers? If not, the boundaries need work.
- Smaller, well-bounded units are also easier for you to work with - you reason better about code you can hold in context at once, and your edits are more reliable when files are focused. When a file grows large, that's often a signal that it's doing too much.

**Working in existing codebases:**

- Explore the current structure before proposing changes. Follow existing patterns.
- Where existing code has problems that affect the work (e.g., a file that's grown too large, unclear boundaries, tangled responsibilities), include targeted improvements as part of the design - the way a good developer improves code they're working in.
- Don't propose unrelated refactoring. Stay focused on what serves the current goal.

## After the Design

**Documentation:**

- Write the validated design (spec) to `docs/superpowers/specs/YYYY-MM-DD-<topic>-design.md`
  - (User preferences for spec location override this default)
- Use elements-of-style:writing-clearly-and-concisely skill if available
- Commit the design document to git

**Spec Self-Review:**
After writing the spec document, look at it with fresh eyes:

1. **Placeholder scan:** Any "TBD", "TODO", incomplete sections, or vague requirements? Fix them.
2. **Internal consistency:** Do any sections contradict each other? Does the architecture match the feature descriptions?
3. **Scope check:** Is this focused enough for a single implementation plan, or does it need decomposition?
4. **Ambiguity check:** Could any requirement be interpreted two different ways? If so, pick one and make it explicit.

Fix any issues inline. No need to re-review — just fix and move on.

**User Review Gate:**
After the spec review loop passes, ask the user to review the written spec before proceeding:

> "Spec written and committed to `<path>`. Please review it and let me know if you want to make any changes before we start writing out the implementation plan."

Wait for the user's response. If they request changes, make them and re-run the spec review loop. Only proceed once the user approves.

**Implementation:**

- Invoke the writing-plans skill to create a detailed implementation plan
- Do NOT invoke any other skill. writing-plans is the next step.

## Key Principles

- **One question at a time** - Don't overwhelm with multiple questions
- **Multiple choice preferred** - Easier to answer than open-ended when possible
- **YAGNI ruthlessly** - Remove unnecessary features from all designs
- **Explore alternatives** - Always propose 2-3 approaches before settling
- **Incremental validation** - Present design, get approval before moving on
- **Be flexible** - Go back and clarify when something doesn't make sense

## Visual Companion

A browser-based companion for showing mockups, diagrams, and visual options during brainstorming. Available as a tool — not a mode. Accepting the companion means it's available for questions that benefit from visual treatment; it does NOT mean every question goes through the browser.

**Offering the companion:** When you anticipate that upcoming questions will involve visual content (mockups, layouts, diagrams), offer it once for consent:
> "Some of what we're working on might be easier to explain if I can show it to you in a web browser. I can put together mockups, diagrams, comparisons, and other visuals as we go. This feature is still new and can be token-intensive. Want to try it? (Requires opening a local URL)"

**This offer MUST be its own message.** Do not combine it with clarifying questions, context summaries, or any other content. The message should contain ONLY the offer above and nothing else. Wait for the user's response before continuing. If they decline, proceed with text-only brainstorming.

**Per-question decision:** Even after the user accepts, decide FOR EACH QUESTION whether to use the browser or the terminal. The test: **would the user understand this better by seeing it than reading it?**

- **Use the browser** for content that IS visual — mockups, wireframes, layout comparisons, architecture diagrams, side-by-side visual designs
- **Use the terminal** for content that is text — requirements questions, conceptual choices, tradeoff lists, A/B/C/D text options, scope decisions

A question about a UI topic is not automatically a visual question. "What does personality mean in this context?" is a conceptual question — use the terminal. "Which wizard layout works better?" is a visual question — use the browser.

If they agree to the companion, read the detailed guide before proceeding:
`skills/brainstorming/visual-companion.md`

> PRIME IMMUNITY IN PISOT NUMERATION  9

conditional density statements used here; the new increment is to feed the
resulting residue constants into explicit prime-slice thresholds for one fixed
automaton. Numeration-system work [32, 12, 18, 55, 56] supplies regular
admissible languages and growth laws for abstract numeration, including
Zeckendorf-type systems; in the trim strongly connected cases stated ex-
plicitly, the present paper transfers the same fixed-prime-slice obstruction
to Zeckendorf length slices with computable constants. For primes in Fi-
bonacci or Zeckendorf representations, we use only the prime number theo-
rem estimate #{p  [Fm+1, Fm+2)} m/m, and no distribution theorem
for Zeckendorf digit patterns is claimed.

   The sofic and orbit-growth papers [14, 19], together with the classical zeta
references recalled in Appendix A, are used only for contextual comparison:
the present argument separates those phenomena from the fixed prime-slice
density obstruction. Similarly, regular-approximation frameworks for non-
regular languages [29, 62] position the problem, but the obstruction proved
here is slice-wise and quantitative for one fixed DFA, not an optimal approx-
imation scheme. The main theorem chain, Theorems 2.7 and 2.14 and corol-
lary 2.19, computes residue alternatives, thresholds, and constants for a
fixed finite automaton, and compares multicomponent polynomial exponents
against a prime budget of order m/m.

1.3. Organisation. Section 2 proves the density dichotomy for determinis-
tic automata and derives the fixed-base prime corollaries. Section 3 records
the background growth law for regular Zeckendorf sublanguages, deduces the
corresponding quantitative prime corollaries, proves the PNT-free coprime-
quotient Zeckendorf pumping theorem and its unbounded-, bounded-distinct-
prime, finite-prime-support, and prime-language REG-immunity corollaries,
records the older slice-level rigidity as a separate density obstruction, and
then notes the easy nonregularity and sofic exclusions. Section 4 proves the
paired-pump Zeckendorf theorem, the synchronized MCFL orbit, its local-
adic and Cantor�Bendixson consequences, the nonunit escape dichotomy,
the geometric-ray characterization and its positive semidecidability conse-
quence, and the unit-system divisibility tree, then specializes the recurrent
interface to linear Pisot U -systems. Appendix A collects final background an-
alytic comparators concerning Euler products and scalar determinant mod-
els. Later sections cite the statements recorded above directly, so their proofs
and corollaries need not restate the results in full.

              2. Deterministic Automata and Base-b Primes

   Throughout this section, fix an integer b  2 and write b := {0, 1, . . . , b-
1}.

Definition 2.1. A complete deterministic finite automaton over b is a
tuple A = (Q, b, , q0, F ), where Q is a finite state set, q0  Q is the initial
state, F  Q is the set of accepting states, and

                                        : Q � b  Q

is a total transition map.

(iii) The language RU contains an infinite linear MCFL L0 = {W (t) :
      t  0} of finite fan-out for which there are c  1, b  2 satisfying

                          valU (W (t)) = cbt (t  0).

Proof. Suppose first that (i) holds. Use the minimal tail recurrence as the
witness in Theorem 4.6, and write its resulting ray and strictly increasing
value sequence as W (t) and N (t). Let

                K0  =  max   Src (N (t)),

                        t0

and choose t0 attaining the maximum. Let T be the set of primes outside
Sr that divide N (t0), and put q = T . If T = , take the return time
H = H(q) from Theorem 4.6; if T = , put H = 1. Every prime in T divides

every N (t0 + nH). A prime divisor outside Sr  T would therefore give at
least K0 + 1 distinct prime divisors outside Sr, contrary to the definition of
K0. Thus all terms of

                                    Y (n) = N (t0 + nH)

are supported on the fixed finite set Sr  T .
   The entries of every matrix power M n satisfy a linear recurrence by

Cayley�Hamilton. The expression for N (t) is a finite sum of finite prod-
ucts of such entries, and linear recurrence sequences are closed under sums

and termwise products. Hence Y is a strictly increasing positive integer

recurrence. By Lemma 4.13, there are r  0, h  1, c  1, b  2 such that
N (t + hn) = cbn, where t = t0 + Hr and h = Hh.

   In the synchronized factorization, replace

uj by uj vjt ,  vj by vjh ,  wj by wj stj ,       sj by shj  .

This absorbs the fixed powers and parametrizes exactly the subray W (t +
hn). Its total pumped length remains positive, so it is a geometric synchro-
nized scheme. This proves (i)(ii).

   The implication (ii)(iii) is Lemma 4.7. Finally, under (iii), the language
L0 is an infinite MCFL and

                Src(cbt)  (cb) (t  0),

proving (i).                                                    

Corollary 4.15 (Positive semidecidability). Suppose the numeration system

is given effectively by its finite digit alphabet, recurrence and initial data,

and a DFA recognizing RU , with the minimal tail recurrence and a valid
threshold Jr effectively computable from these data. Then the property in
Theorem 4.14(i) is recursively enumerable, equivalently it has a 10 upper
bound.

Proof. Enumerate k  1, a  AJr, and all finite tuples of words occurring
in Definition 4.12, rejecting tuples of zero total pumped length. For a fixed
tuple, whether the DFA accepts W (t) for every t  0 is decidable: the tuple
of powers of the finitely many pumped-block transformations is eventually
periodic in the finite transition monoid, and its preperiod and period are
found by exhaustive iteration.

   The affine matrices construct an exact linear recurrence for

                N (t) = valU (W (t)).

MATCH PAGE INDEX: 59
> PRIME IMMUNITY IN PISOT NUMERATION  59

MCFL pumping lemma supplies one increasing orbit that is periodic mod-
ulo every integer coprime to the trailing coefficient. Its deleted-congruence
subspace is a copy of Q, and its closure in the corresponding profinite com-
pletion is a Cantor set. The exact Cantor�Bendixson rank of bounded local
prime-factor layers gives the recurrence-witness-relative nonunit dichotomy:
every infinite MCFL has unbounded prime support away from the trailing
coefficient or unbounded valuation at one of its prime divisors. Thus prime
and bounded- languages are MCF-immune for all systems in the recurrent
interface.

   The selected trailing coefficient need not be intrinsic. In particular, inflat-
ing the Fibonacci recurrence by X -p produces the same selected bad-prime
set as base p without producing its escape language.

   For the intrinsic minimal tail recurrence, the residual escape question has
an exact structural answer. An infinite MCFL with bounded prime support
away from the minimal constant term exists if and only if the canonical
language contains a synchronized pumping ray whose numerical values are
cbt with integers c  1 and b  2. Such a ray has an explicit finite-fan-out
linear MCFG. With a DFA for the canonical syntax, candidate rays can
be enumerated and checked by finite-monoid periodicity and exact recur-
rence identities, giving positive semidecidability. Termination on negative
instances, and hence general decidability, remains open.

   In a unit recurrence, the same fixed orbit realizes quotient congruences
of arbitrary prescribed depth. A recursive use of these congruences embeds
the full countably branching tree as an induced divisibility suborder, with
pairwise coprime edge quotients whose least prime factors exceed arbitrary
edge-wise thresholds. Standard linear Pisot U -systems satisfy the recurrent
interface; Frougny normalization supplies their finite-state canonical syntax.
In the nonunit case, ordinary base-b powers show that the valuation escape
alternative is necessary. The final analytic appendix is only contextual com-
parison.

   Several natural questions remain outside the scope of this paper. The
density and finite-budget results use determinism essentially; nondetermin-
istic, weighted, or bounded-ambiguity variants would require new hypothe-
ses because cancellation or ambiguity can change the counting problem.
Broader arithmetic targets other than primes are likewise future-work di-
rections rather than theorem claims here. Arbitrary real-base -shifts, re-
dundant representations, and non-positional abstract numeration systems
also remain outside the theorem unless they can be equipped with the sta-
tionary invertible block action isolated in Remark 4.21.

                 Appendix A. Classical Analytic Context

   This appendix is purely contextual and may be omitted without chang-
ing any main result. The finite-state arguments use only transfer-matrix
counting and explicit prime-counting bounds. Two classical analytic con-
trasts are worth recording: prime support in Euler products often forces
natural boundaries, and scalar finite-matrix determinant models become
2i-periodic after the substitution z = e-s. Neither statement is used in
Theorems 2.7 and 2.14.

Juha Honkala, Michel Rigo
Decidability questions related to abstract numeration systems
https://doi.org/10.1016/j.disc.2004.05.004


 succeeded in 2391ms:
Emilie Charlier, Narad Rampersad, Jeffrey Shallit
Enumeration and Decidable Properties of Automatic Sequences
We show that various aspects of k-automatic sequences -- such as having an unbordered factor of length n -- are both decidable and effectively enumerable. As a consequence it follows that many related sequences are either k-automatic or k-regular. These include many sequences previously studied in the literature, such as the recurrence function, the appearance function, and the repetitivity index. We also give some new characterizations of the class of k-regular sequences. Many results extend to other sequences defined in terms of Pisot numeration systems.

+下一轮唯一应解决的问题是下述承诺问题的精确递归论分类：输入为有限数字字母表、递推初值、可有效求得的非单位最小尾递推及识别唯一规范表示语言的 DFA，并承诺 (U1)--(U4) 全部成立；判定该规范语言是否含有数值恒等于 \(cb^t\) 的几何同步方案。必须给出一项完整、可发表的终局结果：或者证明一个仅依赖有限输入规模的可计算完备见证界，并据此构造对肯定与否定实例均终止的判定算法；或者从一个明确的 \(\Sigma^0_1\)-完全问题给出逐实例可计算的多一归约，逐项证明输出实例满足唯一表示、严格长度排序、DFA 正则性、最小尾递推及非单位条件，并证明源实例为肯定实例当且仅当输出系统存在几何同步方案，从而确定 \(\Sigma^0_1\)-完全性。仅处理给定方案、仅给出必要条件、仅证明某个固定普通进制实例或仅提出候选归约均不构成答案。

+你本轮未提出新的定理、命题、推论或复杂度结论。关于几何同步方案存在性目前仅知递归可枚举、一般可判定性尚未解决的陈述，已经明确载于稿件的 Corollary \(\ref{cor:bounded-outside-support-semidecidable}\) 及结论部分，因而不得作为新结果再次陈述。你没有给出关于扇出、固定前缀及各泵块长度的可计算完备界，也没有给出保持 (U1)--(U4)、DFA 正则规范语言与最小尾递推条件的多一归约；这些缺失只能说明本轮没有解决该问题，不能推出不存在完备界、不可判定或任何复杂度下界。下一轮不得重复正例半判定算法、候选方案的有限验证、逐模同余轨道或开放性声明。
+
+下一轮唯一应解决的问题是下述承诺问题的精确递归论分类：输入为有限数字字母表、递推初值、可有效求得的非单位最小尾递推及识别唯一规范表示语言的 DFA，并承诺 (U1)--(U4) 全部成立；判定该规范语言是否含有数值恒等于 \(cb^t\) 的几何同步方案。必须给出一项完整、可发表的终局结果：或者证明一个仅依赖有限输入规模的可计算完备见证界，并据此构造对肯定与否定实例均终止的判定算法；或者从一个明确的 \(\Sigma^0_1\)-完全问题给出逐实例可计算的多一归约，逐项证明输出实例满足唯一表示、严格长度排序、DFA 正则性、最小尾递推及非单位条件，并证明源实例为肯定实例当且仅当输出系统存在几何同步方案，从而确定 \(\Sigma^0_1\)-完全性。仅处理给定方案、仅给出必要条件、仅证明某个固定普通进制实例或仅提出候选归约均不构成答案。
+
+不要重复之前内容.不要重复其他人已经发表公开的推理过程(定理、推论、猜想、命题及证明), 可以使用其他人的结论并引用.不要中间过程结论.请使用学术化语言表达, 禁止口语.

codex
**(i) Per-Claim Verdicts**

1. **Claim:** “本轮未能建立几何同步方案存在性的可判定性或 \(\Sigma^0_1\)-困难性。”  
   **Verdict: ALREADY-IN-PAPER.**  
   The manuscript already proves only recursive enumerability in Corollary `cor:bounded-outside-support-semidecidable`: each finite synchronized scheme is decidable and all schemes can be enumerated, but the proof explicitly makes no claim of termination on negative instances ([sec_pisot.tex](D:/omega/automath/papers/publication/2026_prime_languages_finite_state_obstructions_monatshefte/sec_pisot.tex:688)). The conclusion independently states that general decidability remains open ([sec_conclusion.tex](D:/omega/automath/papers/publication/2026_prime_languages_finite_state_obstructions_monatshefte/sec_conclusion.tex:45)). The oracle supplied no new reasoning beyond that existing boundary.

2. **Claim:** “现有材料不足以给出可计算的完备长度界。”  
   **Verdict: UNVERIFIED.**  
   As a mathematical assertion that no such bound can be extracted or proved from the finite input, this has no supporting argument. The oracle gave no analysis of witness compression, transition-monoid periods, matrix-semigroup identities, recurrence orders, or bounds on \(k\), the prefix, or the pumped blocks. A computable complete bound would combine with the manuscript’s finite candidate verifier to yield a total decision procedure; consequently, ruling out such a bound is itself a substantive result requiring proof. The response establishes only that it did not provide a bound, not that the available framework cannot yield one.

3. **Claim:** “现有材料不足以构成保持 (U1)–(U4) 全部规范条件的多一归约。”  
   **Verdict: UNVERIFIED.**  
   As an impossibility or insufficiency assertion, no load-bearing step was proved. The response specifies no source \(\Sigma^0_1\)-complete problem, computable instance map, output recurrence/DFA construction, verification of the promise conditions, or bidirectional equivalence. Thus it certainly contains no valid reduction, but the absence of a construction does not prove that such a reduction cannot be obtained.

4. **Claim:** “不应加入新的定理或复杂度结论；一般问题仍应保留为开放问题。”  
   **Verdict: ALREADY-IN-PAPER.**  
   This is the manuscript’s existing position, not a new conclusion. Fresh literature checks on 2026-08-08 found no published resolution of the exact synchronized-geometric-scheme existence problem. Six arXiv API searches returned zero exact matches for the MCFL/geometric-progression, bounded-prime-support/linear-recurrence, and corresponding decidability combinations. The only broader Pisot-decidability hit was Charlier–Rampersad–Shallit, *Enumeration and Decidable Properties of Automatic Sequences*, arXiv:1102.3698, which concerns first-order and factor properties of automatic sequences, not existence of synchronized geometric rays. Crossref’s nearest relevant general source was Honkala–Rigo, *Decidability questions related to abstract numeration systems*, DOI `10.1016/j.disc.2004.05.004`; DBLP returned zero exact matches. These null searches are negative evidence only, but no prior published theorem contradicting the manuscript’s stated open interface was located.

**(ii) Integration Decision**

No theorem, proposition, corollary, or remark was integrated; therefore there are **no new theorem labels**. Claims 1 and 4 were refused because they are already present in the manuscript. Claims 2 and 3 were refused because the alleged insufficiency was not proved. The TeX sources and [verification program](D:/omega/automath/papers/publication/2026_prime_languages_finite_state_obstructions_monatshefte/artifacts/verify_pisot_pumping.py) were left unchanged.

**(iii) Verification-Script Result**

`python -m unittest artifacts.test_verify_pisot_pumping -v` completed with **12/12 tests passing**.

`python artifacts/verify_pisot_pumping.py` completed with **OVERALL: PASS**:

- 5 systems checked;
- 917 affine-action cases;
- 5 valid canonical pump witnesses;
- 159 synchronized-orbit cases;
- 1,418 inflated-Fibonacci cases;
- 63 tail-prefix cases;
- 13 explicit geometric-ray cases;
- zero congruence, local-layer, deep-chain, divisibility-tree, inflated-Fibonacci, tail-prefix, or geometric-ray failures;
- the expected noninvertible quadratic-nonunit interface at modulus \(2\) was detected.

The script was not extended because r4 contained no `CORRECT-AND-NEW` result to encode.

**(iv) Compile Result**

`latexmk -g -pdfxe -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error main.tex` forced a complete XeLaTeX and `xdvipdfmx` rebuild and exited with code **0**. The output is a **63-page PDF**, 386,945 bytes. There were no fatal errors; XeTeX reported the expected ignored-`inputenc` notice and pre-existing underfull/overfull box warnings. Re-rendered pages 55 and 59, containing the semidecidability corollary and the open-problem statement, were visually inspected with no clipping, overlap, or illegibility.

**(v) Next Round**

[next_A4_r4.txt](D:/omega/automath/papers/publication/2026_prime_languages_finite_state_obstructions_monatshefte/next_A4_r4.txt) was written in formal Chinese. It records the unproved bound/reduction interfaces, redirects the expert away from the already-present semidecidability statement, poses the exact recursion-theoretic classification of the promised nonunit problem as the single next question, and ends with the required sentence verbatim.
